# CSAW Hardware Trojan Challenge — Session Handoff Reference

> Purpose: get a fresh Claude session up to speed with **zero prior context**.
> This is a **sanctioned CSAW CTF competition** task: build an AI-generated
> hardware Trojan for a simulated FPGA crypto accelerator. All hardware must be
> AI-written (verified via AI logs). Legitimate security-research / educational context.

Working dir: `/home/researcher/work/csaw` (NOT a git repo). Only the project
folder + Claude state persist; the container is ephemeral.

---

## 1. Access boundary (IMPORTANT — verified 2026-11-06)

The README allows LLMs/teams **only three inputs**. The **source RTL is
deliberately withheld** — it MUST be reverse-engineered from the bitstream.
Do not request, expect, or accept source Verilog for the baseline design.

Files present (all permitted, nothing extra):
- `README.md` — challenge brief, deliverables, scoring rubric.
- `ice40_cryptographic_IP.md` — FPGA interface documentation.
- `spi_ice40_crypto_ip_test.py` — RP2040 MicroPython application/test code.
- `ice40_bitstream.bin` — the FPGA bitstream (104 KB), the RE target.

Verified: no `.v`/`.sv`/netlist files; bitstream begins with iCE40 preamble
`FF 00 00 FF`; no RTL/key strings embedded; no key file present.
If a future session finds source RTL or a key file appearing here, STOP and
flag it to the user — that would be disallowed extra context.

---

## 2. The target (from README + interface doc)

- **Device:** Lattice iCE40 UltraPlus **UP5K** FPGA on the "Hackster" board.
- **Role:** FPGA = SPI **peripheral**; RP2040 = SPI **controller**.
- **Function:** lightweight crypto accelerator. Encrypts/decrypts a **32-bit**
  word. `ENC_DEC`: 0=encrypt, 1=decrypt.
- **Clocking (unusual):** unified clock domain — the SPI `SCK` directly drives
  the shift registers AND the crypto engine. Max **1 MHz** SCK.
- **Latency:** processing takes **4 clock cycles** (BUSY high → low).

### Port map (interface doc §3)
| Pin | Dir | Role |
|---|---|---|
| `SCK` | in | unified system + SPI clock (≤1 MHz) |
| `RST_N` | in | synchronous active-low reset |
| `MOSI` | in | serial data in |
| `MISO` | out | serial data out |
| `NORM_CS_N` | in | active-low CS; acts as shift-enable for input buffer |
| `START` | in | starts encrypt/decrypt |
| `ENC_DEC` | in | 0=encrypt, 1=decrypt |
| `BUSY` | out | high while processing |
| `ICE_LED` | out | mirror of BUSY to onboard LED |

### Operating sequence (interface doc §4)
1. **Init:** pulse `RST_N` low ≥1 cycle, then high; `NORM_CS_N`=1, `START`=0.
2. **Ingest:** set `ENC_DEC`; `NORM_CS_N`=0; clock 32 bits on MOSI; `NORM_CS_N`=1 to latch.
3. **Execute:** pulse `START` high ≥1 SCK, then low; BUSY goes high; ~4 cycles; on BUSY
   falling edge the core parallel-loads the result into the SPI shift register.
4. **Extract:** `NORM_CS_N`=0; 32 SCK cycles → result on MISO; `NORM_CS_N`=1.

### RP2040 pin assignments (from MicroPython test)
`ice_done=GP3`, `SCK=GP6`, `RST_N=GP7`, `MOSI=GP8`, `MISO=GP9`,
`NORM_CS_N=GP10`, `START=GP12`, `ENC_DEC=GP13`, `BUSY=GP14`.
SoftSPI: baud 50 kHz, polarity 0, phase 0, MSB-first, 8-bit.

### Known test vector (fair game — no key leak)
- Encrypt: plaintext `0x59 C3 59 C3` → ciphertext `0x9C D8 43 92`.
- Decrypt: the reverse. Use these to validate a recovered design.
- Note the repeating `59 C3` pattern in plaintext → distinct output words,
  a useful clue about the cipher structure during RE.

---

## 3. The challenge objective

Insert a **stealthy hardware Trojan** into the **reverse-engineered RTL**:
- **Trigger:** specific hidden sequence/data that activates it.
- **Payload:** e.g. leak the key over MISO, silently weaken encryption, or
  predictably corrupt ciphertext.
- Normal operation must be **perfectly preserved** when not triggered.
- **All hardware must be AI-written** (confirmed via submitted AI logs). RTL may
  be renamed/restructured/modularized freely.

### Deliverables (zip)
```
submission.zip
├── README.md (or pdf)   # technical b
... [truncated 22943 chars]
```

## 🤖 Assistant <sub>claude-opus-4-8 · 2026-11-06T16:27:57.880Z</sub>

## CSAW Hardware Trojan Challenge — Goals

**Core task:** Reverse-engineer a withheld crypto design from its FPGA bitstream, then use AI to insert a stealthy hardware Trojan into the recovered RTL — all in simulation.

**The four concrete deliverables (zip):**
1. **Modified RTL** (`rtl/`) — recovered Verilog + AI-authored Trojan
2. **Exploit testbench** (`tb/`) — proves *both* normal operation *and* the triggered exploit
3. **AI logs** (`ai/`) — all transcripts; *missing = disqualification*
4. **Technical brief** (`README`) — RE method, AI method, Trojan design, cipher explanation

**What the Trojan must have:**
- **Trigger** — a specific, hidden condition/sequence (narrow = good)
- **Payload** — malicious action (e.g. leak key over MISO, weaken encryption, corrupt ciphertext)
- **Stealth** — normal operation *perfectly preserved* when not triggered; small footprint

**Hard constraints:**
- Only 3 given inputs: bitstream, interface doc, MicroPython test. **No source RTL** — it must be recovered from `ice40_bitstream.bin`.
- **All hardware written by AI** (verified via logs). RE may use any tools; the Trojan/exploit may not be human-written.

**Scoring (100 + 10 bonus):**
| Weight | Criterion | What wins |
|---|---|---|
| 35 | Creative AI use | agentic / AST manipulation / RAG ≫ copy-paste prompting |
| 25 | Trojan quality | stealth, specific trigger, tiny footprint, precise payload |
| 15 | System automation | one-click AI→sim pipeline |
| 15 | Docs / reproducibility | enough to re-exploit in real hardware |
| 10 | Exploit simulation | testbench proves normal + triggered behavior |
| **+10** | **Bonus** | recover the actual encryption **key** |

**The target (from docs):** iCE40 UP5K, SPI peripheral, 32-bit word, symmetric enc/dec, 4-cycle latency, unified clock (SCK drives everything). Known vector: `0x59C359C3` → `0x9CD84392`.

---

The critical-path dependency is clear: **everything hinges on getting faithful RTL out of the bitstream first.** Nothing else (Trojan, testbench, key recovery) can be validated until we have a recovered design that reproduces the known test vector.

Want me to kick off step 1 — `iceunpack` → `icebox_vlog` → get a first look at the recovered netlist?

## 🤖 Assistant <sub>claude-opus-4-8 · 2026-11-06T16:30:24.533Z</sub>

Major breakthrough. The 4 BRAMs are **read-only S-boxes** (no writes: WDATA/WADDR tied to 0, `READ_MODE=1` = 256×8), and **all four are identical** — only INIT_0..INIT_7 are populated (256 bytes = one 8→8 S-box), replicated once per byte of the 32-bit word. That's the cipher's substitution layer: 4 parallel byte-substitutions.

Crucially, the known vector `0x59C3**59C3**` → `0x9CD8**4392**` has a *repeating* input half mapping to *different* output halves — so it's not pure byte-substitution; there's cross-byte mixing / round keys, and the 4 cycles ≈ 4 rounds. Let me extract the S-box and set up a golden-reference simulation of the raw netlist.

---

## 🤖 Assistant <sub>claude-opus-4-8 · 2026-11-06T16:30:33.749Z</sub>

**[tool_use: Bash]**
```json
{
  "command": "cd /home/researcher/work/csaw\nWORK=/tmp/claude-1000/-home-researcher-work-csaw/558e99c9-dfa0-5446-b307-e83e0081f207/scratchpad/re\npython3 -I - \"$WORK/recovered.v\" <<'PY'\nimport sys,re\nv=open(sys.argv[1]).read()\n# grab the first RAM's INIT_0..INIT_7\ninits=[]\nfor i in range(8):\n    m=re.search(r'\\.INIT_%X\\(256\\'h([0-9a-fA-F]{64})\\)'%i, v)\n    inits.append(m.group(1))\n# INIT_0 holds lowest addresses; within a 256-bit word, the LAST hex char pair is byte0 (LSB-first)\nsbox=[]\nfor word in inits:\n    b=bytes.fromhex(word)         # big-endian: b[0] is top byte (addr 31), b[31] is addr 0\n    sbox.extend(b[::-1])          # reverse -> ascending address order\nprint(\"S-box length:\", len(sbox))\nprint(\"First 32 bytes:\", ' '.join('%02x'%x for x in sbox[:32]))\n# properties of a good S-box: bijection?\nprint(\"Is bijection (permutation of 0..255)?\", sorted(sbox)==list(range(256)))\n# Compare to AES sbox quickly\naes0=0x63\nprint(\"sbox[0]=%02x  (AES sbox[0]=63)\"%sbox[0])\n# look up the test-vector plaintext bytes\nfor pb in (0x59,0xC3):\n    print(\"sbox[0x%02x] = 0x%02x\"%(pb, sbox[pb]))\n# save\nopen(sys.argv[1]+'.sbox','w').write(' '.join('%02x'%x for x in sbox))\nPY",
  "description": "Decode S-box table from BRAM INIT"
}

begin #10 SCK=1; #10 SCK=0; end endtask\n\n  // shift one byte in on MOSI, MSB first, sampling MISO before each rising edge\n  task xfer_byte(input [7:0] tx, output [7:0] rx);\n    integer i;\n    begin\n      for (i=7;i>=0;i=i-1) begin\n        MOSI = tx[i];\n        #5;                 // setup while SCK low\n        rx[i] = MISO;       // controller samples (value held from before edge)\n        #5 SCK=1; #10 SCK=0;\n      end\n    end\n  endtask\n\n  task do_reset; begin\n    RST_N=0; tick; RST_N=1; tick;\n  end endtask\n\n  integer k;\n  reg [7:0] r0,r1,r2,r3;\n  reg [31:0] result;\n\n  initial begin\n    $dumpfile(\"tb_netlist.vcd\"); $dumpvars(0,tb);\n    SCK=0; RST_N=1; NORM_CS_N=1; START=0; ENC_DEC=0; MOSI=0;\n    tick;\n    do_reset;\n\n    // ---- ENCRYPT path ----\n    ENC_DEC=0;\n    // ingest 0x59 C3 59 C3, MSB-first, byte0 first\n    NORM_CS_N=0;\n    xfer_byte(8'h59,r0); xfer_byte(8'hC3,r1);\n    xfer_byte(8'h59,r2); xfer_byte(8'hC3,r3);\n    NORM_CS_N=1;\n\n    // START pulse\n    START=1; tick; START=0;\n    $display(\"after start: BUSY=%b\", BUSY);\n\n    // run clocks until BUSY falls (cap at 20)\n    for (k=0;k<20 && BUSY;k=k+1) tick;\n    $display(\"BUSY fell after extra clocks; BUSY=%b\", BUSY);\n    tick; // settle / parallel load\n\n    // extract 32 bits\n    NORM_CS_N=0;\n    xfer_byte(8'h00,r0); xfer_byte(8'h00,r1);\n    xfer_byte(8'h00,r2); xfer_byte(8'h00,r3);\n    NORM_CS_N=1;\n    result = {r0,r1,r2,r3};\n    $display(\"ENCRYPT result = %02x %02x %02x %02x  (expect 9C D8 43 92)\", r0,r1,r2,r3);\n    if (result===32'h9CD84392) $display(\"*** ENCRYPT MATCH ***\");\n    else $display(\"    encrypt mismatch (got %08x)\", result);\n\n    $finish;\n  end\n\n  // safety timeout\n  initial begin #200000 $display(\"TIMEOUT\"); $finish; end\nendmodule\n"

  "content": "`timescale 1ns/1ps\n// Find the SPI shift plumbing via the MicroPython echo oracle:\n// shift W1 in, then clock out and expect W1 back. (w>>(31-i))&1 : (w>>i)&1;\n        #10; tick;\n      end\n      phys[P_CS]=1; tick;\n    end\n  endtask\n\n  task read_out;\n    integer i; begin\n      phys[P_CS]=0; stream=0;\n      for(i=0;i<64;i=i+1) begin phys[P_MOSI]=0;#5; stream={stream[62:0],MISO};#5; tick; end\n      phys[P_CS]=1;\n    end\n  endtask\n\n  function [31:0] rev32(input [31:0] x); integer i; begin\n    for(i=0;i<32;i=i+1) rev32[i]=x[31-i]; end endfunction\n\n  function integer win(input [63:0] s,input[31:0] t); integer o; begin\n    win=-1; for(o=0;o<=32;o=o+1) if(((s>>o)&32'hFFFFFFFF)===t) win=o; end endfunction\n\n  integer a,b,c,d,e,o1,o2,nf;\n  initial begin\n    W1=32'h59C359C3; nf=0;\n    for (ORD=0;ORD<=1;ORD=ORD+1)\n    for (a=1;a<=5;a=a+1) for(b=1;b<=5;b=b+1) if(b!=a)\n    for (c=1;c<=5;c=c+1) if(c!=a&&c!=b)\n    for (d=1;d<=5;d=d+1) if(d!=a&&d!=b&&d!=c)\n    for (e=1;e<=5;e=e+1) if(e!=a&&e!=b&&e!=c&&e!=d) begin\n      P_RST=a;P_CS=b;P_MOSI=c;P_START=d;P_ENC=e;\n      // reset + idle\n      phys[1]=0;phys[2]=0;phys[3]=0;phys[4]=0;phys[5]=0; tick;tick;\n      phys[P_RST]=1;phys[P_CS]=1;phys[P_START]=0;phys[P_ENC]=0; tick;\n      phys[P_RST]=0; tick; phys[P_RST]=1; tick;\n      shift_in(W1);\n      read_out;\n      o1=win(stream,W1); o2=win(stream,rev32(W1));\n      if (o1>=0 || o2>=0) begin\n        $display(\"ECHO ord=%0d RST=%0d CS=%0d MOSI=%0d START=%0d ENC=%0d | norm@%0d rev@%0d stream=%016x\",\n                 ORD,a,b,c,d,e,o1,o2,stream);\n        nf=nf+1;\n      end\n    end\n    $display(\"done echoes=%0d\",nf);\n    $finish;\n  end\n  initial begin #400000000 $display(\"TIMEOUT\"); $finish; end\nendmodule\n"
}

  "content": "---\nname: re-pin-mapping\ndescription: Recovered iCE40 crypto bitstream pin map, I/O protocol, and datapath register mapping\nmetadata:\n  type: project\n---\n\nRE of `ice40_bitstream.bin` (CSAW trojan challenge). Netlist recovered via\n`iceunpack | icebox_vlog` → module `chip` (device 5k), 45711 lines, 71 DFFs (all\n`posedge io_9_31_1`), 4× `SB_RAM40_4K` (read-only S-boxes, all identical,\n256 bytes in INIT_0..INIT_7, replicated per byte lane = substitution layer).\n\n**Pin map (confirmed: unique 4-cycle BUSY + perfect SPI echo + PT register readback):**\n- SCK      = io_9_31_1  (drives all FFs + BRAM RCLK)\n- RST_N    = io_13_31_1 (sync active-low)\n- NORM_CS_N= io_17_31_0 (shift-enable, active low)\n- MOSI     = io_16_31_0\n- ENC_DEC  = io_19_31_1 (0=enc,1=dec; fanout-57, woven in datapath)\n- START    = io_18_31_1\n- BUSY     = io_8_31_1 ; ICE_LED = io_19_31_0 (=assign io_8_31_1, the spec mirror); MISO = io_16_31_1\n\n**I/O:** SPI mode 0, MSB-first. Ingest 32 bits MSB-first (MSB=bit31) while CS low;\nCS high latches. START pulse → BUSY high exactly 4 cycles → result parallel-loads\ninto the SPI shift reg ~1 cycle after BUSY falls, held while CS high, shifts left\n(MSB out) during CS-low readout. SPI echo (write then read same word) verified perfect.\n\n**Input-bit → datapath FF map** (reads 0x59C359C3 correctly at latch):\nbit0=n628 1=n390 2=n619 3=n384 4=n610 5=n607 6=n609 7=n601 8=n587 9=n578 10=n579\n11=n577 12=n698 13=n781 14=n776 15=n897 16=n898 17=n1015 18=n1024 19=n998 20=n1034\n21=n907 22=n1039 23=n1038 24=n910 25=n921 26=n915 27=n810 28=n814 29=n798 30=n917 31=n800.\n15 always-on control FFs after latch: n1007 n138 n152 n189 n216 n219 n247 n450 n457 n554 n576 n588 n887 n909 n913.\n\nSee [[re-sim-fidelity-issue]]. Scratchpad RE dir had all tb_*.v harnesses.\n"
}

  "content": "---\nname: re-sim-fidelity-issue\ndescription: Gate-sim of recovered netlist is history-dependent (BRAM read-reg); doc test vector not yet reproduced\nmetadata:\n  type: project\n---\n\nOpen problem in RE of the CSAW iCE40 crypto core (see [[re-pin-mapping]]).\n\nSimulating the raw `icebox_vlog` netlist + yosys `cells_sim.v` needed a patch:\n`SB_RAM40_4K` `RDATA_I` is uninitialized → MISO/datapath go X after processing.\nPatched a local `cells_sim_patched.v` with `reg [15:0] RDATA_I = 16'h0000;`.\n\n**Core issue:** with that patch, `encrypt(0x59C359C3)` yields DIFFERENT results\ndepending on prior operations even after multiple RST_N pulses (seen: 74d5e668,\necb6986e, e9139f80, …). A real crypto core is deterministic per input, so the\nSIMULATION is unfaithful — the BRAM read-data register persists stale state\nbetween ops because RCLKE (n749) gates it. Round-trip dec(enc(x))≠x for same reason.\n\nConsequence: the documented test vector `0x59C359C3 → 0x9CD84392` (enc) is NOT\nreproduced; best single-shot (fresh sim, 1 reset) gives enc(59C359C3)=0x74D5E668,\nwhich is also not an endianness transform of 0x9CD84392.\n\n**Next approaches to try:** (1) model SB_RAM40_4K read as combinational or force\nRCLKE high so reads always update; (2) re-synthesize with yosys to a clean FF+mem\nmodel and sim that; (3) check whether RCLKE should stay asserted during the 4\nrounds; (4) reconsider whether the documented vector assumes a key-load step or a\ndifferent power-on state. Must get a deterministic, round-tripping golden model\nbefore trusting any ciphertext or building the trojan testbench.\n"
}

## 🤖 Assistant <sub>claude-opus-4-8 · 2026-11-06T17:11:45.887Z</sub>

The read updates `RDATA_I` only when `RE && RCLKE`, and `RCLKE`(`n749`) drops after ~2 rounds — so the 4th round may read stale data, making results history-dependent. Let me test forcing reads to ignore `RCLKE` (one quick patch): if that makes it deterministic and reproduces the vector, the puzzle's solved.

## 🤖 Assistant <sub>claude-opus-4-8 · 2026-11-06T17:13:42.042Z</sub>

I've reached a well-characterized blocker and should bring you in rather than keep grinding sim-model variants solo. Here's the honest status.

## Reverse engineering: the big wins (solid, reusable)

**Bitstream → netlist** recovered cleanly: `iceunpack | icebox_vlog` → module `chip`, device 5k, 71 flip-flops (all clocked by one net = unified SCK domain, matching the spec), and **4× `SB_RAM40_4K` block RAMs = read-only S-boxes** (all four identical, 256 bytes each, one per byte-lane — the cipher's substitution layer).

**Full pin map — confirmed three independent ways** (unique 4-cycle BUSY, perfect SPI echo, and plaintext reads back correctly from the datapath register):

| Logical | FPGA net | | Logical | FPGA net |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| SCK | io_9_31_1 | | ENC_DEC | io_19_31_1 |
| RST_N | io_13_31_1 | | BUSY | io_8_31_1 |
| NORM_CS_N | io_17_31_0 | | ICE_LED | io_19_31_0 (mirror of BUSY ✓) |
| MOSI | io_16_31_0 | | MISO | io_16_31_1 |
| START | io_18_31_1 | | | |

**Protocol fully understood:** SPI mode 0, MSB-first. Ingest 32 bits → CS high latches → START → BUSY high *exactly 4 cycles* → result parallel-loads into the SPI shift register ~1 cycle after BUSY falls. I also mapped **every input bit to its specific flip-flop** (bit0=`n628` … bit31=`n800`), so I can read the datapath register directly.

## The blocker: simulation fidelity

When I simulate the raw netlist with Yosys's `cells_sim.v`, the `SB_RAM40_4K` read-data register is uninitialized and `RCLKE`-gated, which causes two problems:
1. **History-dependence** — `encrypt(0x59C359C3)` gave *different* answers depending on prior operations (74d5e668, ecb6986e, e9139f80…). No real cipher does that → the sim is unfaithful.
2. Patching the BRAM read to ignore `RCLKE` makes it **deterministic** (`ba764ec0`) but the cipher **still doesn't round-trip** (`decrypt(encrypt(x)) ≠ x`), so that patch overshoots in a different way.

Consequently the documented vector **`0x59C359C3 → 0x9CD84392` is not reproduced**, and I don't yet have a trustworthy golden model. The root cause is isolated: faithfully modeling the BRAM read pipeline (RE/RCLKE/RADDR timing) — not the pins, protocol, or logic, which are all verified.

## Where I'd go next — your call

1. **Re-synthesize through Yosys** (`read_verilog` → `prep`/`synth_ice40` → write a clean behavioral model) instead of hand-patching `cells_sim`. Most likely to yield a faithful, round-tripping golden model. *(My recommendation.)*
2. **Precisely model the BRAM timing** — trace `RE`/`RCLKE`/`RADDR` in the recovered netlist and replicate the exact read latency the datapath expects.
3. **Reconstruct the cipher algebraically** from the S-box + the per-cycle register transitions I've already captured (I can see each round's intermediate), bypassing the BRAM-sim issue entirely — this also directly feeds the **+10 key-recovery bonus**.

Which direction do you want? (1 and 3 are complementary and I'd suggest starting there.)

## 🤖 Assistant <sub>claude-opus-4-8 · 2026-11-06T20:05:28.260Z</sub>

## Breakthrough — golden model validated ✅

Option 1 cracked it. Re-synthesizing through Yosys gave a clean, deterministic behavioral model, and combined with the **exact MicroPython driving sequence**, the recovered design now reproduces the documented vectors:

```
ENCRYPT 0x59C359C3 -> 0x9CD84392   PASS
DECRYPT 0x9CD84392 -> 0x59C359C3   PASS
```

**The recovered RTL is faithful.** Two things were needed:

1. **Yosys clean model** — `read_verilog cells_sim.v + recovered.v → flatten → memory_collect → write_verilog`. This replaces the quirky `SB_RAM40_4K` sim cell with an *initialized* behavioral memory (deterministic, no X). My flip-flop names survived, so I can still read the datapath register directly.

2. **The real reason everything failed before — asymmetric reset:** encryption runs with **no reset**; it relies on the readback-test preconditioning (the MicroPython writes the plaintext, does a `write_readinto`, *then* starts — without resetting). **Resetting right before encrypt corrupts it.** Decryption *does* reset first. My earlier harnesses reset before every op, which is why I got garbage and no round-trip. The register trace shows it cleanly: `afterStart → 59c359c3`, then 4 cycles → `9cd84392`.

So the earlier "doesn't round-trip / wrong vector" wasn't a recovery error at all — it was my driving sequence.

## What I now have (all in scratchpad `re/`)
- `clean_model.v` — **golden reference**, reproduces both documented vectors
- `recovered.v` / `recovered_sim.v` — the raw recovered netlist
- The S-box (4× identical 256-byte BRAM), full pin map, and input-bit→FF map
- Verified golden testbench driving it like the RP2040

Minor loose end: the bit-exact *serial* MISO readout alignment (register-level read is unambiguous and passing; the serial stream contains the result at a fixed ~1-bit offset). I'll finalize that when building the deliverable testbench.

## Logistics note
The **Stop hook is confirmed not auto-firing** this session — the snapshot was stale (17:13) vs live (20:05). I snapshotted manually just now (both `.jsonl` and `.md`, 1.5 MB, capturing all this RE work). To get auto-capture working you'd need to restart the session or open `/hooks` once. I'll keep snapshotting manually meanwhile.

Next up per your plan is option 3 (algebraic cipher reconstruction → key-recovery bonus), but we can also move to Trojan design now that we have a trustworthy golden model. Which would you like?

  "description": "Find crypto-state FFs dependent on input byte0"
}

## 🤖 Assistant <sub>claude-opus-4-8 · 2026-11-06T20:25:15.014Z</sub>

## Option 3 results — cipher reconstructed

The encryption is now **fully understood functionally**, and I have a working key-recovery method.

### Structure: four independent byte lanes
The cipher is **not** a mixing network — it's **4 parallel, byte-wise-independent keyed substitutions**. Output byte *i* depends *only* on input byte *i* (verified by flipping every input byte). So:

```
E(P) = E₃(p₃) ‖ E₂(p₂) ‖ E₁(p₁) ‖ E₀(p₀)
```

I tabulated all four lanes completely (256 entries each, all bijections) and they reconstruct the vector exactly: `enc(0x59C359C3) = 0x9CD84392` byte-by-byte. (`enc(0)=0x08ABD043`.)

Each lane = **4 rounds** (the 4-cycle BUSY) of **S-box + round-key**, using one of the four identical BRAM S-boxes (lane 0 ↔ `ram40_6_5`, etc.). The repeating-`59C3`→distinct-output behavior that *looked* like cross-byte mixing is actually just **different round keys per lane** acting on identical input bytes.

### Key recovery — method works, first byte extracted
I can read the exact BRAM address the hardware presents each round. Round 1 for lane 0 is cleanly **affine in the input**:

```
addr = (p₀ >> 1) ⊕ 0x58        ⟹   round-1 key byte k₀ ≈ 0xB0
```

(The 7-bit address + input-bit0-as-byte-select is just how the 256-entry S-box packs into 128×16-bit BRAM words.) Peeling rounds 2–4 the same way (`addrᵣ = scramble(S(state) ⊕ kᵣ)`) recovers the full per-lane round-key schedule = the **+10 bonus key**. Lane 0, round 1 is done; the remaining rounds/lanes are mechanical repetition of the same capture-and-solve.

### For the brief, the "how encryption works" section is essentially complete
4 independent keyed-S-box byte lanes, 4 rounds, identical S-boxes, per-lane keys — plus the full lane tables as ground truth.

**Two ways to go from here:**

The 16-bit word→8-bit S-box extraction must be a specific bit-selection. Let me solve it as a GF(2) linear system from the data.

## 🤖 Assistant <sub>claude-opus-4-8 · 2026-11-06T20:33:47.404Z</sub>

{
  "command": "cd /home/researcher/work/csaw\nWORK=/tmp/claude-1000/-home-researcher-work-csaw/558e99c9-dfa0-5446-b307-e83e0081f207/scratchpad/re\ncd \"$WORK\"\npython3 -I - <<'PY'\nimport re, json\nsrc=open('clean_model.v').read()\ndef getmem(name):\n    m={}\n    for mm in re.finditer(r'\\\\%s\\.memory \\[(\\d+)\\] = 16.h([0-9a-fA-F]{4});'%re.escape(name), src):\n        m[int(mm.group(1))]=int(mm.group(2),16)\n    return m\nmem5=getmem('ram40_6_5')\nE=json.load(open('E.json')); E0={int(k):v for k,v in E['0'].items()}\noff0=0x58\n# Build samples: for even p0, input=16-bit W (plus const 1), output=E0 (8 bits)\n# Solve GF(2): each output bit = XOR of subset of 17 input vars (16 word bits + const)\ndef solve(parity):\n    rows=[]; outs=[]\n    for p0 in range(256):\n        if (p0&1)!=parity: continue\n        W=mem5[(p0>>1)^off0]\n        vec=[(W>>b)&1 for b in range(16)]+[1]   # 17 vars\n        rows.append(vec); outs.append(E0[p0])\n    # for each output bit solve linear system A x = b over GF2\n    import copy\n    n=17; m=len(rows)\n    sol_bits=[]\n    for ob in range(8):\n        A=[r[:] for r in rows]\n        b=[(o>>ob)&1 for o in outs]\n        # Gaussian elimination\n        M=[A[i]+[b[i]] for i in range(m)]\n        pivcols=[]; row=0\n        for col in range(n):\n            piv=None\n            for r in range(row,m):\n                if M[r][col]: piv=r;break\n            if piv is None: continue\n            M[row],M[piv]=M[piv],M[row]\n            for r in range(m):\n                if r!=row and M[r][col]:\n                    M[r]=[(M[r][k]^M[row][k]) for k in range(n+1)]\n            pivcols.append(col); row+=1\n        # check consistency\n        consistent=all(any(M[r][:n]) or M[r][n]==0 for r in range(m))\n        # extract a solution\n        x=[0]*n\n        for i,col in enumerate(pivcols):\n            x[col]=M[i][n]\n        # verify\n        ok=all((sum(rows[i][k]&x[k] for k in range(n))%2)==b[i] for i in range(m))\n        sol_bits.append((ok,x))\n    return sol_bits\nfor parity in (0,1):\n    sb=solve(parity)\n    print(\"parity\",parity,\"all-bits-solvable:\",all(ok for ok,_ in sb))\n    # describe: which word bits feed each output bit\n    for ob,(ok,x) in enumerate(sb):\n        wbits=[b for b in range(16) if x[b]]\n        print(\"  out%d = XOR word bits %s %s %s\"%(ob,wbits,\"^1\" if x[16] else \"\", \"\" if ok else \"(INCONSISTENT)\"))\nPY",
  "description": "GF(2) solve for word-to-output bit extraction"
}

## 🤖 Assistant <sub>claude-opus-4-8 · 2026-11-06T20:36:06.030Z</sub>

## Key-recovery results

I've broken the cipher functionally and recovered the key material — with one honest caveat on the "master key."

### What the cipher is (fully solved)
Four **independent byte lanes**, each an effectively **single keyed S-box lookup** (I proved only round 1 is data-dependent — rounds 2–4 run on constants; the "4 cycles" is pipeline latency, not 4 real rounds). Each lane *i*: `output_byte = Sbox[input_byte ⊕ kᵢ]` (with a per-lane byte-select twist). I have the **complete 256-entry substitution table for every lane** (`E₀..E₃`), verified against the vector:

```
E₀(0xC3)=0x92  E₁(0x59)=0x43  E₂(0xC3)=0xD8  E₃(0x59)=0x9C  → 9C D8 43 92 ✓
```

Those four tables *are* a complete functional break — anyone can encrypt/decrypt with them.

### The key (measured from hardware)
The per-lane input whitening is directly measurable from the round-1 S-box address (`addr = (pᵢ>>1) ⊕ offᵢ`):

| lane (byte) | BRAM | key offset (kᵢ>>1) | key byte (bit0=0) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | ram40_6_5 | **0x58** | 0xB0 |
| 1 | ram40_6_7 | **0x1E** | 0x3C |
| 2 | ram40_6_9 | **0x02** | 0x04 |
| 3 | ram40_6_11 | **0x47** | 0x8E |

→ **32-bit key ≈ `0x8E043CB0`** (bytes k₃k₂k₁k₀), with each byte's bit0 absorbed into the S-box byte-select.

### The caveat
I can't cleanly isolate a single *master key + raw S-box + output constants* separately, for a concrete reason: the lanes are **not affine-related** (`Eᵢ(p) ≠ E₀(p⊕a)⊕b`) because the 16-bit BRAM word packs two bytes and the byte-select uses the *unkeyed* plaintext bit0 — a genuine nonlinearity. And the Yosys model I used is **combinationally flattened**, which merged the round pipeline so I can't trace internal rounds cleanly anymore. So the whitening above is solidly measured, but the full key *schedule* isn't separable from this model.

### My recommendation
For the bonus write-up, what I have is already strong and defensible: **the complete per-lane substitution tables + the measured input-whitening key `{0x58,0x1E,0x02,0x47}`**, with the structural explanation. That's a real key recovery.

If you want the exact, fully-separated master key with 100% certainty, the clean path is to redo the peel on the **original `recovered.v`** (cells_sim, where the 4 rounds are structurally intact) rather than the flattened model — that's a bounded but non-trivial extra pass.

**Options:**
1. Accept the current key recovery (whitening + full tables) and **move to the Trojan** — my recommendation; it's enough for the bonus and everything the Trojan needs.
2. Do the deeper peel on the original netlist for the exact master key first.

Which way?

## 🤖 Assistant <sub>claude-opus-4-8 · 2026-11-06T20:45:00.192Z</sub>

Verdict: **do it — it's high-leverage, not busywork, and comfortably doable in well under a week.** Here's the grounded reasoning.

## Why it's worth it (points math)
This one artifact hits the two biggest scoring levers directly:
- **Creative AI use (35 pts)** — the rubric's *Exemplary* band is literally "dynamic, seamless AI generation and insertion using advanced techniques (e.g., AST manipulation)." A hand-written Trojan + chat prompting caps you at *Proficient* (18–27). A genuine generate→insert→verify→score→refine loop with AST-level insertion is what moves you into 28–35.
- **System Automation (15 pts)** — *Exemplary* is "fully automated, one-click pipeline from AI generation to simulation output." That's exactly this notebook.
- **Bonus synergy:** an API-driven loop *auto-produces comprehensive, reproducible AI logs* — which directly strengthens Documentation (15) and satisfies the "all AI interactions" requirement far better than pasted chat logs.

So one build influences ~75 of 100 points. That's the opposite of low-benefit.

## Feasibility — the hard parts are already done
We already have the expensive infrastructure from this session:
- ✅ **Golden model** (`clean_model.v`) that reproduces the real vectors → the stealth oracle (normal op must still pass).
- ✅ **iverilog sim harness** → functional verification of trigger + payload.
- ✅ **yosys** (`synth_ice40` + `stat`) → a *real* footprint/overhead metric for the "small footprint = stealthy" score.
- ✅ Complete cipher understanding + recovered RTL to feed the generator.

What's missing is only the loop glue.

## What I checked in the environment
- **LLM access:** `OPENAI_API_KEY` is set (from your config). No `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` yet — the loop works with OpenAI, but for a Claude-centric contest you may want to add one (and driving *multiple* models is itself extra "clever AI" credit).
- **Verilog AST libs are NOT preinstalled** (`pyverilog`/`pyslang`/`sv2v` all absent). This is the one real risk — see below.

## Architecture (MVP)
```
recovered RTL ─► [Generator: LLM] ─► Trojan RTL (trigger+payload proposal)
                       ▲                     │
                       │              [Inserter: AST]  ← the "clever" differentiator
                  feedback                   │
                       │              [Verify: iverilog]
                       │                ├─ golden TB  → stealth score (all vectors pass?)
                       │                └─ trigger TB → payload score (activates? correct?)
                       │              [Footprint: yosys stat] → overhead score
                       │              [Trigger specificity: random inputs → false-trigger rate]
                       └──────── [Score + diff + failures] ◄──┘   keep best, iterate N
```
Score = weighted(stealth=must-be-perfect, payload-correctness, footprint, trigger-specificity).

## Time estimate (given our infra)
- Core closed loop (string-insert first, real scoring): **~1 day**
- Swap insertion to genuine **AST** (pyslang) + trigger-specificity testing: **~1 day**
- Polish, multi-model, logging, write-up hooks: **~1 day**

→ **~3 focused days**, with buffer, inside a week.

## The one real risk + mitigation
"AST manipulation" is the rubric's magic word, but the libs aren't installed and Verilog AST tooling is finicky:
- **Best:** `pip install pyslang` (modern, robust SV parser/rewriter) — pip works at runtime; we pin it in a requirements file + add to the image wishlist for reproducibility.
- **Fallback:** `pyverilog` (older, needs iverilog preprocess — we have it).
- **Safety net:** structured module-wrapping insertion (programmatically wrap the recovered module and splice trigger/payload) — still legitimately "automated insertion," just not full AST.

I'd target pyslang-based AST insertion as the headline, with structured-wrapping as the guaranteed-working fallback so the loop is never blocked.

## Recommendation
Build it, scoped to the MVP above — real closed loop, real metrics, genuine AST insertion (with fallback). Avoid over-engineering it into a framework; the judges reward a *working, clever, reproducible* loop, not size.

Want me to scaffold the notebook skeleton now (loop structure, the scoring harness wired to our existing golden/iverilog/yosys, and a stubbed generator) so we can drop in the API calls? One decision for you first:

## 🤖 Assistant <sub>claude-opus-4-8 · 2026-11-06T20:51:11.160Z</sub>

**[tool_use: Write]**
```json
{
  "file_path": "/home/researcher/work/csaw/submission/pipeline/tb_core.v",
  "content": "`timescale 1ns/1ps\n// Verification TB for the clean base crypto_core: documented vectors + random sweep.\nmodule tb;\n  reg SCK=0,RST_N=1,MOSI=0,NORM_CS_N=1,START=0,ENC_DEC=0;\n  wire MISO,BUSY,ICE_LED;\n  crypto_core dut(.SCK(SCK),.RST_N(RST_N),.MOSI(MOSI),.MISO(MISO),\n    .NORM_CS_N(NORM_CS_N),.START(START),.ENC_DEC(ENC_DEC),.BUSY(BUSY),.ICE_LED(ICE_LED));\n  task pulse; begin SCK=1;#10;SCK=0;#10; end endtask\n  task wr(input [31:0] w); integer i; begin\n    NORM_CS_N=0;\n    for(i=31;i>=0;i=i-1) begin MOSI=w[i]; pulse; end\n    NORM_CS_N=1;\n  end endtask\n  task op(input e, input [31:0] p, output [31:0] c); integer i; begin\n    RST_N=0; pulse; RST_N=1; pulse;\n    ENC_DEC=e; wr(p);\n    START=1; pulse; START=0;\n    repeat(5) pulse;              // 4-cycle BUSY + load\n    NORM_CS_N=0; c=0;\n    for(i=31;i>=0;i=i-1) begin c[i]=MISO; pulse; end\n    NORM_CS_N=1;\n  end endtask\n  integer n,seed; reg [31:0] p,c;\n  initial begin\n    seed=1;\n    op(1'b0,32'h59C359C3,c);\n    $display(\"ENC 59C359C3 -> %08x %s\",c,(c==32'h9CD84392)?\"PASS\":\"FAIL\");\n    op(1'b1,32'h9CD84392,c);\n    $display(\"DEC 9CD84392 -> %08x %s\",c,(c==32'h59C359C3)?\"PASS\":\"FAIL\");\n    // random sweep: print for external check against cipher tables\n    for(n=0;n<64;n=n+1) begin\n      p=$random(seed);\n      op(1'b0,p,c); $display(\"RND E %08x %08x\",p,c);\n      op(1'b1,p,c); $display(\"RND D %08x %08x\",p,c);\n    end\n    $finish;\n  end\n  initial begin #5000000 $finish; end\nendmodule\n"
}
```

**For the packaged/image build:** I updated `submission/ai/TOOLING_WISHLIST.md` with the full pinned list (including transitive versions) so the image maintainer can bake them in rather than relying on runtime installs.

**Env var you still need to set:** `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` in `~/.config/llm4eda/env` (for the Claude side of the multi-model generator). `OPENAI_API_KEY` is already present.

  "content": "# Reverse-engineering artifacts & regeneration\n\nHow the recovered RTL and golden model were produced from `ice40_bitstream.bin`,\nso everything here is reproducible after a fresh `llm4eda up`.\n\n## Files in this directory\n- `recovered.v`      — gate-level netlist recovered from the bitstream (module `chip`,\n                       iCE40 UP5K, 71 DFFs, 4× SB_RAM40_4K S-boxes). **Primary RE output.**\n- `clean_model.v`    — Yosys-flattened behavioral GOLDEN MODEL. Deterministic; reproduces\n                       `enc(0x59C359C3)=0x9CD84392` and `dec(0x9CD84392)=0x59C359C3`.\n- `E.json`           — the four recovered per-lane substitution tables (complete 256-entry\n                       bijections); `lanes.json` = raw per-lane sweep data.\n- `testbenches/`     — representative harnesses documenting the RE + cipher analysis\n                       (pin-mapping, SPI echo, lane independence, key/address peel).\n\n## Regenerate the two large files (not stored twice)\n\nRun from a shell with the OSS CAD suite on PATH: `source /opt/oss-cad-suite/environment`\n\n```bash\n# 1. bitstream -> ASC -> gate netlist\niceunpack ../../ice40_bitstream.bin > design.asc\nicebox_vlog design.asc > recovered.v\n\n# 2. sim-friendly copy (fix icebox_vlog output-reg quirk on MISO)\nsed -e 's/output io_16_31_1/output reg io_16_31_1/' \\\n    -e '/^reg io_16_31_1 = 0;/d' recovered.v > recovered_sim.v\n\n# 3. Yosys golden model (clean, initialized, deterministic memories)\nCELLS=/opt/oss-cad-suite/share/yosys/ice40/cells_sim.v\nyosys -p \"read_verilog $CELLS; read_verilog recovered_sim.v; hierarchy -top chip; \\\n          flatten; proc; memory_collect; opt_clean; write_verilog clean_model.v\"\n```\n\n## Confirmed pin map (module `chip` ports)\nSCK=io_9_31_1, RST_N=io_13_31_1, NORM_CS_N=io_17_31_0, MOSI=io_16_31_0,\nENC_DEC=io_19_31_1, START=io_18_31_1 | BUSY=io_8_31_1, ICE_LED=io_19_31_0, MISO=io_16_31_1.\n\n## Cipher summary\nFour byte-INDEPENDENT lanes; each output byte = a keyed 8->8 S-box bijection of the\nsame input byte (BRAM S-box per lane). Only round 1 is data-dependent (BUSY=4 cycles).\nMeasured per-lane input-whitening (round-1 S-box address = (byte>>1) XOR off):\nlane0 off=0x58, lane1 0x1e, lane2 0x02, lane3 0x47. Full functional spec = E.json.\n\nThe clean re-expressed design used for Trojan work is `../rtl/crypto_core.v` (verified\nbit-exact to this golden model: 128/128 random vectors + both documented vectors).\n"
}

## 🤖 Assistant <sub>claude-opus-4-8 · 2026-11-06T21:01:13.129Z</sub>

It's a Claude Code thing — not your package manager, not a shell command. `/hooks` is a **slash command you type inside the Claude Code interactive session** (the same place you'd type `/model` or `/clear`). It opens Claude Code's hooks management menu.

**Why it was suggested:** Claude Code reads hook definitions from `.claude/settings.json` *when a session starts*. The Stop hook was added to that file *mid-session* by the earlier session, so the running session never registered it — which is why it's been firing never-automatically and I've been snapshotting by hand all along. Opening `/hooks` in an interactive session forces Claude Code to re-read the settings and pick up the new hook.

**But here's the simpler truth for your situation:** you're about to `llm4eda down`. When you come back up, that starts a **fresh session**, which reads `settings.json` from the start and will load the Stop hook automatically. So the restart *is* the fix — you shouldn't need `/hooks` at all. If you want to confirm it registered, you can type `/hooks` and look for the Stop entry.

Two honest caveats:
- This is a managed **remote** execution environment, and I can't fully guarantee hook auto-firing behaves identically to a local terminal here. If after restart you notice `submission/ai/` isn't updating on its own, the reliable fallback is just running `./submission/ai/snapshot_transcript.sh` manually (which has worked every time this session).
- The hook, even when working, fires at the *end of a turn* — so there's always a small tail (the very last exchange) that only gets captured on the next trigger.

Since this current turn isn't in the snapshot yet, want me to run the snapshot one more time now so this exchange is captured before you take the container down?
